Although no single crisis occurred, a pattern of small but recurring lapses—like overlooking important emails in a chain or mis-sequencing steps in a complex rollout—started to erode his confidence and prompted quiet feedback from a trusted physician partner that he seemed \"less on top of the details\" than before. cares deeply about the work, he decided to consult a psychiatrist after reading about adult ADHD in an article on healthcare leadership performance.\n- Chief Complaint: In long meetings or when I’m working through complex plans, my mind keeps drifting off and I miss important details even though I’m really trying to pay attention.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): Even with my color‑coded spreadsheets, I still end up sending out reports with things like a column summed wrong or a date range off by a week because I’ve skimmed instead of really reading the numbers before I hit send, and my team will catch it and email me the correction.\n- S005 (Organization_Difficulty): When I’m juggling a new clinic rollout plus three community projects, I’ll sit at my desk not sure what to tackle first, then end up bouncing between email, the project plan, and my calendar without actually finishing anything in a logical order. \n- S006 (Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance): If I know I have to sit down and write a long proposal or review a thick policy document, I’ll keep finding “quick” things to do—checking on a nurse manager, answering a couple more emails—because the thought of focusing on one dense task for an hour or two just feels exhausting. \n- S008 (Distractibility): In those long strategy meetings, I’ll start out really trying to focus, but after about 10 or 15 minutes my mind drifts to things like whether I confirmed the speaker for Saturday’s health fair or what I’m making for dinner, and then I realize I’ve completely missed what was just decided about a key deadline. \n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): My phone is full of half‑written reminder notes because if I don’t write things down immediately I’ll forget to return a call, pay a bill, or show up to a community board meeting, and even with the reminders I still occasionally realize I’ve missed something when someone follows up asking where I am. \n- S015 (Excessive_Talking): Once I get going about a project—especially in smaller meetings or community groups—I’ll catch myself talking over people and giving way more detail than anyone asked for, and I only realize I’ve been dominating the conversation when I notice others looking at the clock or not jumping in.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- actually done, and my parents used to joke that I’d forget my head if it wasn’t attached.\n- functional_impairment: Over the past couple of years, my missed details and overlooked emails have led to coworkers quietly double-checking my work and taking over pieces of projects, and I’ve started turning down new leadership opportunities because I’m worried I can’t reliably keep everything straight Although no single crisis occurred, a pattern of small but recurring lapses—like overlooking important emails in a chain or mis-sequencing steps in a complex rollout—started to erode his confidence and prompted quiet feedback from a trusted physician partner that he seemed \"less on top of the details\" than before. Concerned that his long-standing tendency to lose track of things is becoming more evident now that his responsibilities are higher-stakes, and wanting to understand why his focus slips even when he cares deeply about the work, he decided to consult a psychiatrist after reading about adult ADHD in an article on healthcare leadership performance.\n- Chief Complaint: In long meetings or when I’m What brings you in?\n\nPatient: I work as a healthcare operations manager, and I've been really struggling with my focus lately. During long meetings or when I’m working through complex plans, my mind keeps drifting off and I miss important details even though I’m really trying to pay attention. It's starting to impact my work, so I wanted to get some professional advice.\n\nDoctor: In addition to the focus problems, have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm depressed or losing interest at all. I actually care deeply about my work and my community projects, but I just feel really frustrated and overwhelmed by how hard it is to stay focused lately.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or distractibility problems as a child or teenager, before age 12, or is this a new problem that started only recently?\n\nPatient: These issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but always daydreaming, losing worksheets, and forgetting to turn in homework. My parents used to joke that I’d forget my head if it wasn't attached.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and forgetfulness problems happen in more than one setting, such as both at work and at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, it's definitely not just limited to my day job. At home, I'm constantly forgetting to pay bills on time and my phone is cluttered with half-written reminders just so I don't lose track of basic personal errands. It also spills into my volunteer roles, where I've completely missed community board meetings or gotten totally sidetracked while trying to plan our local health fairs.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of hyperactivity or impulsivity, such as feeling restless, fidgeting, interrupting others, or acting before thinking?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel physically restless or fidgety, but I definitely struggle with talking too much and interrupting others. In smaller meetings or community groups, I tend to dominate conversations, talk over people, and give way more detail than anyone asked for. I usually only realize I'm doing it when I notice others checking the clock or not jumping in.\n\nDoctor: Do these talking too much and interrupting behaviors cause problems in your daily life, such as at work, in relationships, or in meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely causes some social awkwardness, particularly in my smaller committee and community meetings. I often catch myself talking over people and giving way too much detail, only realizing I've been dominating the conversation when I notice others looking at the clock. It leaves me feeling quite embarrassed and worried that I'm frustrating the very people I want to work closely with.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention problems and interrupting/talking-too-much behaviors cause noticeable difficulties at school, at home, or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely caused some issues even back then. In elementary school, my teachers frequently noted on my report cards that I was bright but always daydreaming, losing my worksheets, and forgetting to turn in completed homework. At home, my constant forgetfulness led my parents to joke that I'd forget my head if it wasn't attached to my shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel physically restless or unable to sit still, such as fidgeting, tapping, leaving your seat, or feeling driven to move around?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really experience physical restlessness, fidgeting, or a need to constantly move around. I've never really been someone who taps their feet or feels the urge to get up and pace. My struggles are much more about my mind wandering rather than my body needing to move.

It's starting to impact my work, so I wanted to get some professional advice.\n\nDoctor: In addition to the focus problems, have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm depressed or losing interest at all. I actually care deeply about my work and my community projects, but I just feel really frustrated and overwhelmed by how hard it is to stay focused lately.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or distractibility problems as a child or teenager, before age 12, or is this a new problem that started only recently?\n\nPatient: These issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. In elementary school, my teachers frequently noted on my report cards that I was bright but always daydreaming, losing my worksheets, and forgetting to turn in completed homework. At home, my constant forgetfulness led my parents to joke that I'd forget my head if it wasn't attached to my shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel physically restless or unable to sit still, such as fidgeting, tapping, leaving your seat, or feeling driven to move around?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience physical restlessness, fidgeting, or a need to constantly move around. I've never really been someone who taps their feet or feels the urge to get up and pace. My struggles are much more about my mind wandering rather than my body needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do these focus and impulsive talking problems make it hard for you to complete tasks on time or lead to mistakes at work, home, or in daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely cause mistakes; I often send out spreadsheets with incorrect date ranges or wrong column sums because I skimmed them, and I'll constantly bounce between different tasks without actually finishing them in a logical order. It's gotten to the point where coworkers quietly double-check my work and take over pieces of projects, which has even led me to turn down new leadership roles because I'm worried I can't reliably keep everything straight anymore."

My struggles are much more about my mind wandering rather than my body needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do these focus and impulsive talking problems make it hard for you to complete tasks on time or lead to mistakes at work, home, or in daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely cause mistakes; I often send out spreadsheets with incorrect date ranges or wrong column sums because I skimmed them, and I'll constantly bounce between different tasks without actually finishing them in a logical order. I usually only realize I've done it when I notice others looking at the clock."

During long meetings or when I’m working through complex plans, my mind keeps drifting off and I miss important details even though I’m really trying to pay attention. It's starting to impact my work, so I wanted to get some professional advice.\n\nDoctor: In addition to the focus problems, have you been feeling down, depressed, or losing interest or pleasure in things most days lately?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I'm depressed or losing interest at all. I actually care deeply about my work and my community projects, but I just feel really frustrated and overwhelmed by how hard it is to stay focused lately.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or distractibility problems as a child or teenager, before age 12, or is this a new problem that started only recently?\n\nPatient: These issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, my teachers would write on my report cards that I was bright but always daydreaming, losing worksheets, and forgetting to turn in homework. My parents used to joke that I’d forget my head if it wasn't attached.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and forgetfulness problems happen in more than one setting, such as both at work and at home or in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely, it's definitely not just limited to my day job. At home, I'm constantly forgetting to pay bills on time and my phone is cluttered with half-written reminders just so I don't lose track of basic personal errands. It also spills into my volunteer roles, where I've completely missed community board meetings or gotten totally sidetracked while trying to plan our local health fairs.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have symptoms of hyperactivity or impulsivity, such as feeling restless, fidgeting, interrupting others, or acting before thinking?\n\nPatient: I don't really feel physically restless or fidgety, but I definitely struggle with talking too much and interrupting others. In smaller meetings or community groups, I tend to dominate conversations, talk over people, and give way more detail than anyone asked for. I usually only realize I'm doing it when I notice others checking the clock or not jumping in.\n\nDoctor: Do these talking too much and interrupting behaviors cause problems in your daily life, such as at work, in relationships, or in meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely causes some social awkwardness, particularly in my smaller committee and community meetings. I often catch myself talking over people and giving way too much detail, only realizing I've been dominating the conversation when I notice others looking at the clock. It leaves me feeling quite embarrassed and worried that I'm frustrating the very people I want to work closely with.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention problems and interrupting/talking-too-much behaviors cause noticeable difficulties at school, at home, or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely caused some issues even back then. In elementary school, my teachers frequently noted on my report cards that I was bright but always daydreaming, losing my worksheets, and forgetting to turn in completed homework. At home, my constant forgetfulness led my parents to joke that I'd forget my head if it wasn't attached to my shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel physically restless or unable to sit still, such as fidgeting, tapping, leaving your seat, or feeling driven to move around?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience physical restlessness, fidgeting, or a need to constantly move around. I've never really been someone who taps their feet or feels the urge to get up and pace. My struggles are much more about my mind wandering rather than my body needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do these focus and impulsive talking problems make it hard for you to complete tasks on time or lead to mistakes at work, home, or in daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely cause mistakes; I often send out spreadsheets with incorrect date ranges or wrong column sums because I skimmed them, and I'll constantly bounce between different tasks without actually finishing them in a logical order. It's gotten to the point where coworkers quietly double-check my work and take over pieces of projects, which has even led me to turn down new leadership roles because I'm worried I can't reliably keep everything straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often make impulsive decisions or act without thinking, such as blurting things out, interrupting others, spending impulsively, or doing things that later cause problems?\n\nPatient: I don't really struggle with impulsive spending or reckless actions, but I definitely have issues with conversational impulsivity. During meetings or community projects, I often catch myself talking over people, dominating the conversation, and sharing way too much detail. I usually only realize I've done it when I notice others looking at the clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel very restless inside, like you have to move or cannot sit still, even if you are not physically fidgeting?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience that kind of internal restlessness or a constant need to move. My struggles are almost entirely with my mind drifting off rather than any kind of physical or internal restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when your energy felt unusually high, you needed much less sleep, and you were more talkative or impulsive than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never had periods of unusually high energy or a decreased need for sleep like that. My talkativeness is really more about getting overly excited about work projects I care about, rather than feeling manic or impulsive."
  }

I actually care deeply about my work and my community projects, but I just feel really frustrated and overwhelmed by how hard it is to stay focused lately.\n\nDoctor: Did you have similar attention or distractibility problems as a child or teenager, before age 12, or is this a new problem that started only recently?\n\nPatient: These issues actually go all the way back to my childhood. It's gotten to the point where coworkers quietly double-check my work and take over pieces of projects, which has even led me to turn down new leadership roles because I'm worried I can't reliably keep everything straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often make impulsive decisions or act without thinking, such as blurting things out, interrupting others, spending impulsively, or doing things that later cause problems?\n\nPatient: I don't really struggle with impulsive spending or reckless actions, but I definitely have issues with conversational impulsivity. During meetings or community projects, I often catch myself talking over people, dominating the conversation, and sharing way too much detail. I usually only realize I've done it when I notice others looking at the clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel very restless inside, like you have to move or cannot sit still, even if you are not physically fidgeting?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience that kind of internal restlessness or a constant need to move. My struggles are almost entirely with my mind drifting off rather than any kind of physical or internal restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when your energy felt unusually high, you needed much less sleep, and you were more talkative or impulsive than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never had periods of unusually high energy or a decreased need for sleep like that. My talkativeness is really more about getting overly excited about work projects I care about, rather than feeling manic or impulsive.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes, lose things, or forget everyday tasks because you’re distracted or not fully following through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I often make careless mistakes at work, like sending out spreadsheets with incorrect dates or formula errors because I skimmed them instead of reading carefully. My talkativeness is really more about getting overly excited about work projects I care about, rather than feeling manic or impulsive.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes, lose things, or forget everyday tasks because you’re distracted or not fully following through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I often make careless mistakes at work, like sending out spreadsheets with incorrect dates or formula errors because I skimmed them instead of reading carefully. I also constantly forget everyday tasks like returning phone calls or paying bills, which is a habit that goes back to my childhood when I was always losing school worksheets and forgetting homework.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and impulsivity symptoms happen in a way that clearly interferes with your life in more than one area, such as work, home, and social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, they absolutely affect multiple areas of my life. At work, colleagues have started quietly double-checking my work, and I've even turned down new leadership roles because of my self-doubt. Meanwhile, in my personal and community life, I constantly forget to pay bills, miss board meetings despite reminders, and tend to talk over people or dominate conversations in social groups."

In smaller meetings or community groups, I tend to dominate conversations, talk over people, and give way more detail than anyone asked for. I usually only realize I'm doing it when I notice others checking the clock or not jumping in.\n\nDoctor: Do these talking too much and interrupting behaviors cause problems in your daily life, such as at work, in relationships, or in meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it definitely causes some social awkwardness, particularly in my smaller committee and community meetings. I often catch myself talking over people and giving way too much detail, only realizing I've been dominating the conversation when I notice others looking at the clock. It leaves me feeling quite embarrassed and worried that I'm frustrating the very people I want to work closely with.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did these attention problems and interrupting/talking-too-much behaviors cause noticeable difficulties at school, at home, or with friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely caused some issues even back then. In elementary school, my teachers frequently noted on my report cards that I was bright but always daydreaming, losing my worksheets, and forgetting to turn in completed homework. At home, my constant forgetfulness led my parents to joke that I'd forget my head if it wasn't attached to my shoulders.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel physically restless or unable to sit still, such as fidgeting, tapping, leaving your seat, or feeling driven to move around?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience physical restlessness, fidgeting, or a need to constantly move around. I've never really been someone who taps their feet or feels the urge to get up and pace. My struggles are much more about my mind wandering rather than my body needing to move.\n\nDoctor: Do these focus and impulsive talking problems make it hard for you to complete tasks on time or lead to mistakes at work, home, or in daily responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, they definitely cause mistakes; I often send out spreadsheets with incorrect date ranges or wrong column sums because I skimmed them, and I'll constantly bounce between different tasks without actually finishing them in a logical order. It's gotten to the point where coworkers quietly double-check my work and take over pieces of projects, which has even led me to turn down new leadership roles because I'm worried I can't reliably keep everything straight anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often make impulsive decisions or act without thinking, such as blurting things out, interrupting others, spending impulsively, or doing things that later cause problems?\n\nPatient: I don't really struggle with impulsive spending or reckless actions, but I definitely have issues with conversational impulsivity. During meetings or community projects, I often catch myself talking over people, dominating the conversation, and sharing way too much detail. I usually only realize I've done it when I notice others looking at the clock.\n\nDoctor: Do you also often feel very restless inside, like you have to move or cannot sit still, even if you are not physically fidgeting?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really experience that kind of internal restlessness or a constant need to move. My struggles are almost entirely with my mind drifting off rather than any kind of physical or internal restlessness.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of several days or more when your energy felt unusually high, you needed much less sleep, and you were more talkative or impulsive than usual?\n\nPatient: No, I've never had periods of unusually high energy or a decreased need for sleep like that. My talkativeness is really more about getting overly excited about work projects I care about, rather than feeling manic or impulsive.\n\nDoctor: Do you often make careless mistakes, lose things, or forget everyday tasks because you’re distracted or not fully following through?\n\nPatient: Yes, I often make careless mistakes at work, like sending out spreadsheets with incorrect dates or formula errors because I skimmed them instead of reading carefully. I also constantly forget everyday tasks like returning phone calls or paying bills, which is a habit that goes back to my childhood when I was always losing school worksheets and forgetting homework.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and impulsivity symptoms happen in a way that clearly interferes with your life in more than one area, such as work, home, and social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, they absolutely affect multiple areas of my life. At work, colleagues have started quietly double-checking my work, and I've even turned down new leadership roles because of my self-doubt. Meanwhile, in my personal and community life, I constantly forget to pay bills, miss board meetings despite reminders, and tend to talk over people or dominate conversations in social groups.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these attention problems and interrupting/talking too much have been present continuously since childhood, or do they come and go in episodes?\n\nPatient: They have definitely been a continuous, long-standing pattern since childhood rather than coming in episodes. Even back in elementary school, I was always daydreaming and losing worksheets, but the struggles have become much more noticeable over the last two years as my responsibilities expanded."

Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.
